=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an array nums consisting of positive integers.
We call two integers x and y in this problem almost equal if both integers can become equal after performing the following operation at most once:

Choose either x or y and swap any two digits within the chosen number.

Return the number of indices i and j in nums where i < j such that nums[i] and nums[j] are almost equal.
Note that it is allowed for an integer to have leading zeros after performing an operation.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [3,12,30,17,21]
Output: 2
Explanation:
The almost equal pairs of elements are:

3 and 30. By swapping 3 and 0 in 30, you get 3.
12 and 21. By swapping 1 and 2 in 12, you get 21.


Example 2:

Input: nums = [1,1,1,1,1]
Output: 10
Explanation:
Every two elements in the array are almost equal.

Example 3:

Input: nums = [123,231]
Output: 0
Explanation:
We cannot swap any two digits of 123 or 231 to reach the other.

 
Constraints:

2 <= nums.length <= 100
1 <= nums[i] <= 10^6

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def countPairs(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

class Solution:
    def countPairs(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        def swapped_values(x):
            s = str(x)
            res = {x}
            arr = list(s)
            for i in range(len(arr)):
                for j in range(i+1, len(arr)):
                    arr[i], arr[j] = arr[j], arr[i]
                    res.add(int(''.join(arr)))
                    arr[i], arr[j] = arr[j], arr[i]
            return res

        reachable = [swapped_values(x) for x in nums]
        ans=0
        for i in range(len(nums)):
            for j in range(i+1,len(nums)):
                if nums[j] in reachable[i] or nums[i] in reachable[j]:
                    ans +=1
        return ans

If y's padded string equals a valid swap, then okay. But if we count using padded strings and all swaps, could false positives? Example x=1, y=10. Pad to length2: "01" and "10". All swaps of "01": swap pos0,1 -> "10" =10. But original x=1 cannot become 10 (only one digit), and y=10 can become 1 by swapping "10" -> "01" =1. So pair is almost equal. If we count y in reachable(x) using padded all swaps, we'd say x can become y, but actually y can become x. Still pair almost equal. For pair counting, false positive maybe not if relation symmetric? But if we use padded all swaps to define reachability, it may be symmetric? Let's see.

Pad all numbers to fixed length L. For any string s of length L with leading zeros, swapping any two digits is invertible. If y's padded string can be obtained from x's padded string by one swap, then x's padded string can be obtained from y's padded string by the same swap. Thus relation is symmetric. Does this relation exactly match almost equal? Need test.

Take x=3, y=30, L=2: s_x="03", s_y="30"; swap s_y -> "03", so yes. x=3 can become 30? In padded relation, yes (swap "03" -> "30"), but original x cannot. However pair is almost equal, so padded relation says true. Good.

Take x=12, y=21, L=2: "12" -> "21" yes. Good.

Take x=123, y=231, L=3: "123" swaps: 213,321,132; not 231. "231" swaps: 321,132,213; not 123. false. Good.

Take x=1, y=10, L=2: "01" swap -> "10", true. Pair almost equal because 10->1. Good.

Take x=1, y=100, L=3: "001" and "100". Can "001" swap pos0,2 -> "100" =100. Pair almost equal? Original 100 can swap first 1 and last 0 -> "001"=1. Yes true. Padded relation true.

Take x=10, y=100, L=3: "010" and "100". Can "010" swap pos0,2 -> "010" (same), pos0,1 -> "100" =100. So padded relation true. Original pair? 10 can swap digits "10" -> "01"=1, not 100. 100 can swap digits: "100" -> "010"=10 (swap first 1 and second 0) yes! So pair almost equal. Good.

Take x=12, y=120, L=3: "012" and "120". Can "012" swap pos0,2 -> "210"=210, pos0,1 -> "102"=102, pos1,2 -> "021"=21. Not 120. "120" swaps: "210", "021"=21, "102"=102. Not 12. Pair false? Wait "120" swap first digit 1 and last digit 0 -> "021"=21, not 12. Swap first digit 1 and second digit 2 -> "210"=210. Swap second digit 2 and last digit 0 -> "102"=102. None 12. So false. Padded relation false. Good.

Take x=12, y=102, L=3: "012" and "102". "012" swap pos0,1 -> "102"=102. Original 12 cannot become 102. 102 can swap first 1 and second 0 -> "012"=12. Pair almost equal. Padded relation true. Good.

It seems padding to fixed length and considering one swap in padded strings might exactly capture almost equal? Need prove? Let's investigate.

Original number x has decimal representation without leading zeros, length len_x. Operation swaps any two digits in this representation. The resulting string may have leading zeros, numeric value may have fewer digits. If we pad x to length L with leading zeros, the original digits occupy positions L-len_x ... L-1. A valid swap of two original digits corresponds to swapping two positions within that suffix. The resulting padded string has leading zeros. If we allow swapping any two positions in the padded string, including leading zeros, we may produce strings that are not obtainable by a valid swap. But perhaps any such string is still obtainable by swapping two digits in the other number? For pair counting, if padded relation says true, does original almost equal always true? Need find counterexample.

Let x=1, y=2, L=2: "01" and "02"; one swap? "01" swap pos0,1 -> "10"=10, not "02"; "02" -> "20"=20, not "01"; no. false. Original false.

Potential false positive: padded relation true due to swapping a leading zero of shorter number with a digit, producing a number with more significant digits, while the longer number cannot be swapped to the shorter? But because swap is invertible in padded strings, if padded x can become padded y by one swap, then padded y can become padded x by same swap. Does that imply original y can become original x? Not necessarily because the swap in padded y may involve leading zeros that are not original digits of y. But maybe if padded x can become padded y by swapping a leading zero of x, then y has fewer significant digits? Let's analyze.

Let L fixed. Original x length a, original y length b. Padded x has L-a leading zeros then digits of x. Padded y has L-b leading zeros then digits of y. Suppose padded x -> padded y by swapping positions p,q. If both p,q are within the suffix of x (original digits), then original x can become y (possibly with leading zeros) exactly. If one or both positions are in the leading zero prefix of x, then original x cannot perform that swap. But since padded y has y's digits in suffix of length b. The resulting string after swap equals padded y. What can we infer about lengths? If swap involves a leading zero of x moving into a significant position, then x's significant digit moves to leading zero prefix, so the resulting string has fewer significant digits? Actually padded y may have leading zeros. The number of leading zeros in padded y is L-b. The swap result's first nonzero position determines b. If x had length a, padded x has L-a leading zeros. Swapping a leading zero with a digit in suffix can reduce/increase length? Let's construct possible false positive.

Example L=3, x=12 (a=2, padded "012"), y=102 (b=3, padded "102"). Padded x swap pos0 (leading zero) and pos1 (digit 1) -> "102" = y. Original x=12 cannot become 102. But original y=102 can swap pos0 and pos1 -> "012" =12. So pair true. The padded relation true due to x leading zero swap, but original almost equal true via y. Maybe always if padded relation true, at least one original can perform the swap? Let's test.

Suppose padded x -> padded y by swapping positions p,q. If p,q are both in x's significant suffix, original x can. If not, then at least one of p,q is in x's leading zero prefix. Then the other is in x's significant suffix (unless both in prefix, then result same? If both leading zeros, no change, would imply x=y? Actually swapping two leading zeros yields same padded string, so y=x; then original equal). If one leading zero and one significant digit, then after swap, a significant digit moves to a leading position, so the resulting string has fewer leading zeros than x? Let's calculate: x length a, padded x has prefix zeros count L-a. Swap a zero at position r < L-a with a digit at position s >= L-a. The digit moves to position r, which is before all original significant digits. The zero moves to position s. The resulting string's first nonzero digit is at r (since r < L-a and digit nonzero), so number of leading zeros becomes r, which is < L-a. Thus y's length b = L - r, which is > a? Since r < L-a => b = L-r > a. So y is longer than x. The swap in padded y: positions r and s. In y's original representation, since y length b = L-r, the leading zeros count is L-b = r. Position r is the first digit of y (nonzero). Position s? s >= L-a. Since b = L-r, suffix of y starts at r. Position s is within y's suffix? Need s < L? yes. Is s >= r? yes. So both positions r and s are within y's significant suffix? Wait y's suffix starts at r, so position r is the first significant digit (not leading zero). Position s >= L-a > r? Since L-a > r, yes s > r. So both positions are in y's original significant digits. Thus original y can swap those two digits to get padded x. Therefore pair is almost equal. Great. If both swapped positions in x's prefix? impossible unless x=y. If one leading zero and one significant, the other number is longer and can perform the swap. So padded relation exactly captures almost equal? Need also consider if padded relation false but original almost equal true? If original x can become y, then padded x can become padded y by same swap (positions in suffix), so padded relation true. If original y can become x, then padded y -> padded x, so padded relation true. Thus padded relation iff almost equal. Nice.

This suggests a simpler and more symmetric algorithm: pad all numbers to a fixed length L (e.g., 7 or max digits) and count pairs whose padded strings are equal or one swap apart. But wait, if we pad to fixed length L = max(len(nums)) maybe enough? Need ensure any valid swap with leading zeros can be represented. If L is max length among nums, then for a shorter number x, its padded representation has leading zeros. If x can become y where y has same length as x or shorter, padded y has leading zeros; okay. If y is longer than x, then original x cannot become y, but original y can become x; padded relation may be true via swapping leading zero of x. Need L >= max length to represent y. If L = max length, okay. If L = 7 (max value 1e6 length 7), also okay. If L = max(len(nums)), all numbers padded to same L. If a number has length equal to L, no leading zeros. If another shorter, leading zeros. Good.

Could use padded strings and count pairs where Hamming? Actually one swap in string of length L. Need count pairs i<j such that s_i and s_j are equal or can be made equal by swapping any two characters in one string. Since relation symmetric in padded strings. We can count by grouping? For n=100, brute force comparing strings and checking if one swap apart is easy. But maybe we can use reachable sets of padded strings? Let's see.

If we use padded strings, almost equal iff s_i and s_j are at swap distance <=1 in the sense that there exists a single transposition transforming one string into the other. Because transposition is symmetric. We can check for pair: if s_i == s_j -> true. Else, find positions where they differ. If exactly 2 positions differ and s_i[d1]==s_j[d2] and s_i[d2]==s_j[d1], then one swap. If more than 2 differences, false. Is that sufficient? Let's test with padded strings. Example x=3 ("03"), y=30 ("30") L=2: differ positions 0,1; s_i[0]=0=s_j[1], s_i[1]=3=s_j[0], true. x=12 ("12"), y=21 ("21"): differ positions 0,1; true. x=123 ("123"), y=231 ("231"): differ all 3 positions? Compare "123" vs "231": pos0 1 vs2, pos1 2 vs3, pos2 3 vs1. More than 2, false. Good. x=12 ("012"), y=102 ("102") L=3: differ pos0 (0 vs1), pos1 (1 vs0), pos2 (2 vs2 same). Exactly 2 differ and swapped, true. Original almost equal via 102->12. Good.

Wait, what about strings with duplicate characters and more than 2 positions differ but one swap could still make equal? A single swap changes exactly two positions (unless swapping equal chars no change). If after swap strings equal, then the two strings must differ at exactly the two swapped positions, and at those positions the characters are swapped. If there are duplicate characters, could they differ at more than 2 positions but a swap of two positions makes equal? No, a swap changes only two positions, so the set of differing positions between original and target after swap is subset? Let's reason: If s and t differ at positions D. Applying one swap to s changes characters at positions p,q. For s' to equal t, all positions outside {p,q} must already match, so D subset {p,q}. If p or q not in D, swapping equal chars? If p not in D, s[p]=t[p]; after swap with q, s'[p]=s[q], need equal t[p]=s[p], so s[q]=s[p]. This could happen with duplicates, and D could be {q} only? Example s="aa", t="ab"? D={1}; swap p=0,q=1 in s -> "aa", not "ab"; no. Let's formalize: If D size >2, impossible because swap changes at most two positions. If D size =1, impossible because swap changes two positions but if one swapped position already matched, the other might change to match while the matched position changes away unless equal chars; maybe possible with duplicates? Example s="ab", t="aa": D={1}; swap pos0 and1 in s -> "ba", not "aa"; swap pos1 with pos? only pos0. No. If s="aa", t="ab": D={1}; swap pos0,1 -> "aa", not "ab". So no. If D size=0 equal. If D size=2, need swapped. So condition works.

But is padded string relation exactly "s_i and s_j differ at exactly two positions with swapped chars" or equal? Let's test with x=1, y=100, L=3: s_x="001", s_y="100". D positions 0 (0 vs1),1 (0 vs0? wait s_y[1]=0, same),2 (1 vs0). D={0,2}; s_x[0]=0=s_y[2], s_x[2]=1=s_y[0], true. Good.

This padded string difference method is elegant and O(n^2 * L) with L<=7. But wait, is L fixed to max length of nums enough? Suppose nums = [1, 1000000] L=7. s_1="0000001", s_1000000="1000000". So padded difference works.

But is it always true that if padded strings differ at exactly two positions with swapped chars, then original numbers are almost equal? We argued via transposition symmetry and one of original numbers can perform the swap. Let's prove more rigorously for ourselves:
Let s,t be length L padded strings of x,y. Suppose t can be obtained from s by swapping positions p,q. If p,q are both within the significant digits of x (i.e., positions >= L-len(x)), then x can become y. If not, at least one of p,q is in the leading zero prefix of x. Since s has zeros in prefix. If both p,q in prefix, s=t (because swapping zeros), contradiction to distinct? If s=t then x=y, almost equal. If exactly one in prefix, then the other is a significant digit of x. After swap, t has a nonzero digit at the prefix position, so t has fewer leading zeros than s. Thus len(y) > len(x). Now consider swapping p,q in t. Since t has fewer leading zeros, both p and q are within the significant digits of y? Need check q (the original significant digit position) is definitely within y's significant suffix? t's significant suffix starts at p (the prefix position where nonzero moved). q > p? Since q was in x's significant suffix, q >= L-len(x). p < L-len(x), so q > p. Thus q is within y's suffix. p is the first significant digit of y. So both positions are in y's original digits. Therefore y can swap p,q to get s. Thus pair almost equal. Good. If both p,q in x's significant suffix, x can. If one in prefix, y can. So yes.

Thus we can implement countPairs using padded strings and compare difference positions. This avoids generating reachable sets and int conversion, and is O(n^2 * L). It also handles duplicates. But is there any case where padded strings differ at exactly two positions with swapped chars but the swap in the longer number involves a leading zero in the longer number? Wait if one in prefix of x, we said y can swap p,q. But p is first significant digit of y, q is within y's suffix. So both are original digits of y. Good. If both p,q in x's significant suffix, x can. If both in prefix, equal. So fine.

Need decide which algorithm to present. The starter code expects List[int]. We can implement either reachable set or padded difference. The padded difference method is concise and robust. Need ensure no issue with L = max(len(str(num))). If L=0? nums length >=2, positive, so L>=1. Use zfill(L).

Let's think about if L should be max length of original nums or maybe max length after operation? Operation preserves length of chosen number, but leading zeros can make numeric value shorter. Now, if nums length up to 100, O(n^2) fine. We can implement:

class Solution:
    def countPairs(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        L = max(len(str(x)) for x in nums)
        strs = [str(x).zfill(L) for x in nums]
        ans = 0
        for i in range(len(strs)):
            for j in range(i+1, len(strs)):
                a, b = strs[i], strs[j]
                diff = []
                for k in range(L):
                    if a[k] != b[k]:
                        diff.append(k)
                        if len(diff) > 2:
                            break
                if len(diff) == 0:
                    ans += 1
                elif len(diff) == 2:
                    p, q = diff
                    if a[p] == b[q] and a[q] == b[p]:
                        ans += 1
        return ans

This is very concise. Need ensure if len(diff)>2 break; if break early, len(diff)>2 so false. Good.

Could also use `if len(diff) == 2 and ...`; if len(diff)>2 false. Good.

Potential hidden nuance: Leading zeros after operation allowed, but original numbers are positive and no leading zeros. We pad for comparison. Good.

Now, let's consider if padded difference method might count a pair where strings differ at exactly two positions with swapped chars but the original numbers are not almost equal because the longer number's swap would require swapping two digits but one of them is not in the chosen number due to leading zeros? Now, could there be a pair where original numbers are almost equal but padded strings differ at exactly two positions with swapped chars? We argued yes. Let's prove: If x can become y by swapping positions p,q in original x. Let s,t padded. The swap in original x corresponds to swapping the same positions in s (since original digits are suffix). The resulting string equals t. Thus s and t differ at p,q (unless swapped digits equal? If swapped digits equal, no change, so x=y and s=t). If swapped digits differ, then s[p]=t[q], s[q]=t[p], and all other positions same. So diff exactly 2. If y can become x, symmetric. If x=y, diff 0. So padded difference condition necessary and sufficient.

for i in range(n):
    for j in range(i+1,n):
        s = strs[i]
        t = strs[j]
        diff = [k for k in range(L) if s[k] != t[k]]
        if len(diff) == 0:
            ans += 1
        elif len(diff) == 2:
            p,q = diff
            if s[p] == t[q] and s[q] == t[p]:
                ans += 1

So y's length = L-p. Good. If p is not in x's prefix but in x's significant suffix, then x can swap. If p is in x's prefix and q also in x's prefix impossible. Now, let's consider if there is a pair where padded strings differ at exactly two positions with swapped chars but p in x's prefix and q in x's significant suffix, but t[q] = s[p] = 0. So q is a zero in y. Swapping p (nonzero) with q (zero) in y yields s. Valid. If q was also nonzero? swapped condition says t[q]=s[p]=0, so q zero. Good.

Now, let's think about if original y can perform the swap when p and q are both in y's original digits but one of them is a leading zero in y's original representation? No, original representation doesn't include leading zeros; y's original digits are exactly t[p..L-1] because t has p leading zeros. p is first digit, nonzero. q is after p. So both are in original representation. Good.

Now, let's decide final code. We can include comments maybe:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def countPairs(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        L = max(len(str(x)) for x in nums)
        s = [str(x).zfill(L) for x in nums]
        ans = 0
        n = len(nums)
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(i+1, n):
                a, b = s[i], s[j]
                diff = [k for k in range(L) if a[k] != b[k]]
                if len(diff) == 0:
                    ans += 1
                elif len(diff) == 2:
                    p, q = diff
                    if a[p] == b[q] and a[q] == b[p]:
                        ans += 1
        return ans
```
This is final.

But the directed reachability is not symmetric. Counting pairs by checking if y in reachable[x] for each x and summing over all x would count each symmetric pair twice? So known solution must handle differently.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def countPairs(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        L = max(len(str(x)) for x in nums)
        strs = [str(x).zfill(L) for x in nums]
        ans = 0
        n = len(nums)

        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(i + 1, n):
                a, b = strs[i], strs[j]
                diff = [k for k in range(L) if a[k] != b[k]]

                if len(diff) == 0:
                    ans += 1
                elif len(diff) == 2:
                    p, q = diff
                    if a[p] == b[q] and a[q] == b[p]:
                        ans += 1

        return ans
```
